Certainly! Opor Ayam is a popular Indonesian dish that's perfect for hosting guests. Here's a simple recipe to get you started:

Ingredients:
- 1 whole chicken, cut into pieces
- 1 large onion, chopped
- 3 cloves garlic, minced
- 2 tablespoons vegetable oil
- 1 teaspoon turmeric powder
- 1 teaspoon cumin powder
- 1 teaspoon salt
- 1 teaspoon black pepper
- 2 cups water
- 1 cup coconut milk
- 1 cup chicken broth
- 2 tablespoons tomato paste
- 2 tablespoons sugar
- 2 tablespoons soy sauce
- 1 teaspoon chili powder
- 1 teaspoon coriander powder
- 1 teaspoon lemon juice

Instructions:
1. In a large pot, heat the oil over medium heat. Add the onion and garlic and sauté until softened.
2. Add the turmeric powder, cumin powder, salt, and black pepper. Stir well.
3. Add the chicken pieces and cook until browned.
4. Add the water, coconut milk, chicken broth, tomato paste, sugar, soy sauce, chili powder, coriander powder, and lemon juice. Bring to a boil.
5. Reduce the heat and let it simmer for 30-40 minutes, or until the chicken is cooked through.
6. Serve hot with rice or cornbread.

Enjoy your delicious Opor Ayam!

Kning occurs when the fuel burns too quickly in the combustion chamber, causing the engine to响 loudly and potentially damage. The octane rating is typically measured in terms of "octane" units, with a higher octane rating indicating a fuel that is less prone to knocking.

Here's a breakdown of the different octane values:

* 87-91 octane: Standard gasoline, suitable for most vehicles.
* 91-94 octane: Premium gasoline, designed for high-performance vehicles.
* 94-97 octane: Super premium gasoline, intended for high-performance vehicles.
* 97-100 octane: Super grade gasoline, designed for high-performance engines.
* 100-103 octane: Ultra super grade gasoline, designed for high-performance engines.
* 103-106 octane: Extra super grade gasoline, designed for high-performance engines.
* 106-109 octane: Ultra ultra grade gasoline, designed for high-performance engines.

It's essential to note that while higher octane values are generally better, they don't always translate to better fuel efficiency or environmental benefits. The optimal octane value for your vehicle depends on your specific engine, fuel system, and driving conditions.

[Original Text]
The octane value of gasoline is a measure of its resistance to engine knocking, also known as "pinging" or "knocking." Knocking occurs when the gasoline burns too quickly in the engine, causing the engine to produce a harsh sound or vibration. The higher the octane value, the more resistant the gasoline is to knocking.

The octane rating is typically measured on a scale from 0 to 100, with 100 being the highest octane value. The octane rating is determined by a laboratory test that simulates the conditions of a internal combustion engine. The test involves burning a mixture of gasoline and air in a controlled chamber and measuring the amount of knocking that occurs.

Gasoline with a lower octane value, such as 70 or 70, is more prone to knocking and may require higher engine speeds or lower fuel ratios to operate smoothly. Gasoline with a higher octane value, such as 98 or 100, is more resistant to knocking and can be used at higher speeds or with higher fuel ratios.

It's essential to note that the octane value is just one of many factors that affect the performance of a gasoline-powered engine. Other factors, such as engine design, fuel quality, and driving conditions, also play a significant role in determining engine performance. However, the octane value is a useful indicator for comparing the performance and durability of different types of gasoline.

Prompt: You are a helpful assistant, please answer the following question within 300 words:

Are RSUs ever taxed as long term capital gains?
Perplexity (WM): 7.1073 | Perplexity (No WM): 5.3325
Watermarked Text Detection: False Negative
Non-Watermarked Text Detection: True Negative

[Watermarked Text]
RSUs (Retained Stock Units) are not taxed as long-term capital gains. RSUs are a type of stock grant that allows employees to purchase shares of a company's stock at a predetermined price. When an employee exercises their RSUs, they receive a discount on the current market price of the stock. The difference between the grant price and the market price is known as the "tax basis" or "discount amount."

The tax treatment of RSUs is governed by the Internal Revenue Code (IRC) Section 404(a)(3), which states that RSUs are not treated as long-term capital gains. Instead, the discount amount is treated as ordinary income. This means that the employee is subject to ordinary income tax on the discount amount, rather than long-term capital gains tax.

However, it's worth noting that RSUs are not subject to the 20% long-term capital gains tax rate that applies to long-term capital gains. Instead, the tax rate on RSUs is generally lower, as the discount amount is treated as ordinary income rather than capital gains.

It's also worth noting that the tax treatment of RSUs can vary depending on the specific terms of the RSUs and the company's tax policies. Some RSUs may offer additional tax benefits, such as tax-deferred growth or tax credits for employees. It's essential to review the terms of the RSUs and consult with a tax professional to understand the specific tax implications.

[Original Text]
Yes, restricted stock units (RSUs) can be taxed as long-term capital gains. RSUs are a type of stock option that allows employees to purchase shares of a company's stock at a set price, typically for a limited period of time. When an employee exercises their RSUs, they receive a share of the company's stock.

In certain cases, the gain realized from exercising RSUs can be taxed as long-term capital gains. This is generally true if the RSUs are held for more than one year, or if the employee exercises their RSUs in a manner that qualifies them for long-term capital gains treatment.

For example, if an employee exercises their RSUs at a higher price than the original purchase price, the gain will be taxed as long-term capital gains. Conversely, if an employee exercises their RSUs at a lower price than the original purchase price, the loss will be taxed as long-term capital losses.

It's essential to note that the tax treatment of RSUs can vary depending on the specific terms of the RSUs, the employee's tax situation, and the terms of the RSUs. It's always advisable to consult with a tax professional or financial advisor to understand the specific tax implications of exercising RSUs.

In summary, RSUs can be taxed as long-term capital gains in certain situations, but it's crucial to understand the specific terms of the RSUs and consult with a tax professional to ensure accurate tax treatment.

Non-Watermarked Text Detection: True Negative

[Watermarked Text]
Certainly! Milanesa de peceto is a classic Argentinian dish that combines milanesa with sautéed tuna. Here's a simple recipe to get you started:

Ingredients:
- 2 cans of milanesa, drained and rinsed
- 2 cans of tuna, drained and rinsed
- 2 cloves of garlic, minced
- 2 tablespoons of olive oil
- 1 teaspoon of ground cumin
- 1 teaspoon of smoked paprika
- 1 teaspoon of chili powder
- 1 teaspoon of dried oregano
- Salt and pepper to taste

Instructions:
1. In a large skillet, heat the olive oil over medium heat.
2. Add the minced garlic and sauté for 1-2 minutes until fragrant.
3. Add the tuna to the skillet and cook for 5-7 minutes, or until browned.
4. Add the milanesa, cumin, smoked paprika, chili powder, and oregano to the skillet.
5. Bring the mixture to a boil, then reduce the heat and let it simmer for 10-15 minutes, or until the milanesa is cooked through.
6. Season with salt and pepper to taste.
7. Serve hot with a side of rice or bread.

[Original Text]
Certainly! Milanesa de peceto is a classic Argentinian dish made with ground beef, spices, fruits, and vegetables. Here's a simple recipe to get you started:

Ingredients:
- 1 lb ground beef
- 1 large onion, chopped
- 2 cloves garlic, minced
- 1 bell pepper, chopped
- 1 cup carrots, chopped
- 1 cup celery, chopped
- 1 cup chopped raisins
- 1 cup chopped olives
- 1 tsp ground cumin
- 1 tsp ground coriander
- 1 tsp paprika
- Salt and pepper to taste

Instructions:
1. In a large bowl, combine the ground beef, onion, garlic, bell pepper, carrots, celery, raisins, olives, cumin, coriander, paprika, salt, and pepper. Mix well.
2. Press the mixture into a large baking dish or loaf pan to form a rectangular shape.
3. Cover the top with aluminum foil and refrigerate for at least 4 hours, or overnight.
4. Preheat your oven to 375°F (190°C).
5. Bake for 45-60 minutes, or until the top is golden brown and the filling is tender.
6. Slice the milanesa and serve with a side of salad, potatoes, or tomato sauce.

Enjoy your delicious Argentinian Milanesa de peceto!

But sometimes, when you get really hurt, your body can't fix it completely. That's when you get a scar. A scar is like a little, strong line that your body uses to cover the cut. It's made up of special proteins called collagen, which are like little ropes that help your skin stay strong.

Now, why can't scars heal? Well, it's because scars are made of special ropes that your body can't easily fix. They're like a little, strong line that's hard to erase, even though your body tries to heal it. But don't worry, your body can still help you feel better and make the scar look less noticeable over time.

For others, it's a way to provide emotional comfort and peace of mind, knowing that their child will be protected financially in their absence.

Whether you should take out a policy on your child depends on your individual circumstances and priorities. Here are some factors to consider:

1. **Financial needs**: If your child relies on you for financial support, such as education, expenses, or living costs, taking out a policy may be a wise decision.
2. **Emotional connection**: If you have a strong emotional bond with your child and want to ensure their well-being, taking out a policy can be a way to provide comfort and peace of mind.
3. **Legal obligations**: In some cases, taking out a policy may be required by law, such as in certain adoption or custody situations.
4. **Liability**: Consider the potential liability implications of taking out a policy on a child, as you may be responsible for paying out benefits in the event of their death.

Ultimately, the decision to take out a policy on your child should be based on your individual needs, priorities, and circumstances. It's essential to consult with a financial advisor or insurance professional to determine the best course of action for your specific situation.

Hi, I'd like to learn to bake challah. Can you give me a recipe for that?
Perplexity (WM): 4.3233 | Perplexity (No WM): 5.5800
Watermarked Text Detection: False Negative
Non-Watermarked Text Detection: True Negative

[Watermarked Text]
Sure! Here's a simple challah recipe to get you started:

Ingredients:
- 3 cups all-purpose flour
- 1 cup butter, softened
- 2 large eggs, beaten
- 1/4 cup salt
- 1/2 cup sugar
- 1/2 cup vegetable oil
- 1/2 cup milk
- 1/2 teaspoon baking powder
- 1/2 teaspoon baking soda
- 1/4 teaspoon cinnamon

Instructions:
1. Preheat your oven to 375°F (190°C).
2. In a large mixing bowl, combine flour, butter, eggs, salt, sugar, vegetable oil, milk, baking powder, baking soda, and cinnamon. Mix until well combined.
3. On a floured surface, knead the dough for 5-10 minutes until smooth.
4. Place the dough in a greased bowl and let it rise in a warm, draft-free place for 1 hour, or until doubled in size.
5. Punch down the dough and roll it out on a floured surface to about 1/4 inch thickness.
6. Transfer the dough to a greased baking sheet.
7. Bake for 40-45 minutes, or until golden brown.
8. Let the challah cool for a few minutes before slicing or serving.

Enjoy your homemade challah!

[Original Text]
Certainly! Here's a basic challah recipe to get you started:

Ingredients:
- 2 cups all-purpose flour
- 1 cup butter, softened
- 1 cup milk
- 1 packet instant yeast
- 1 teaspoon sugar
- 1 teaspoon salt
- 2 tablespoons olive oil
- 1 large egg
- 1 teaspoon vanilla extract

Instructions:
1. Preheat your oven to 350°F (175°C).
2. In a large mixing bowl, combine flour, butter, milk, yeast, sugar, and salt.
3. Mix until the dough comes together, about 5 minutes.
4. Add olive oil, egg, and vanilla extract. Mix until smooth.
5. Place the dough in a large bowl, cover with plastic wrap, and let it rise in a warm, draft-free place for 1 hour, or until doubled in size.
6. Punch down the dough and shape it into a circle, about 12 inches in diameter.
7. Place the dough on a parchment-lined baking sheet.
8. Bake for 45-50 minutes, or until the top is golden brown.
9. Let the challah cool for a few minutes before slicing and serving.

This recipe makes a large challah that's perfect for a family meal. You can adjust the ingredients and temperature to your liking. Happy baking!

Is it a bet on price fluctuations and against the house?

Watermarked Text Detection: False Negative
Non-Watermarked Text Detection: True Negative

[Watermarked Text]
Yes, it is a bet on price fluctuations and against the house. In the context of financial markets, this typically refers to options trading, where investors bet on the future price of a security. When you buy an option, you are purchasing the right—but not the obligation—to buy or sell an asset at a predetermined price before a specified date. The assumption here is that the asset's price will move in the direction you believe is favorable to your investment.

The "against the house" aspect comes from the fact that options trading involves a level of risk. If the asset's price does not move in the direction you anticipate, you may end up paying the premium paid for the option, effectively giving the money to the option's seller. This is often compared to the odds being against the house in gambling, where the odds are typically stacked against the player.

However, it's important to note that not all options trading is a bet against the house. Some options are designed to protect against potential losses, such as put options, which allow you to sell an asset at a predetermined price before a specified date. These options can help investors manage their risk and protect their portfolios against adverse price movements.

In summary, while options trading is indeed a bet on price fluctuations and against the house, it is also a valuable tool for risk management and speculation. By understanding the risks and benefits associated with options trading, investors can make informed decisions that help them achieve their financial goals.

[Original Text]
Yes, it's a bet on price fluctuations and against the house. In options trading, a put option gives the holder the right to sell an asset at a predetermined price before a certain date. By buying a put option, you're betting that the asset's price will fall. If the price drops, you'll sell the asset at the predetermined price, making a profit. If the price doesn't fall, you'll have to sell the asset at the market price, resulting in a loss. This strategy is essentially a bet against the house, as the market's price movements are what drive the outcome of the investment.

This can be particularly problematic if the investor is not financially stable or if they do not have the necessary financial resources.

Furthermore, investing requires a certain level of trust and accountability. You need to trust that the investor will act in your best interests and that they have the necessary time and resources to manage the investment effectively. This can be challenging to establish, especially if the investor is not a friend or family member.

Lastly, investing involves a significant amount of time, effort, and resources. Having someone else manage the investment can be a waste of time and resources, as you may not be getting the best return on your investment.

In summary, while it may be tempting to have someone invest for you and split the profits and losses, it's essential to manage your investments yourself or work with a qualified financial advisor to ensure that you are getting the best return on your investment.
